New Rendition

53 And they led Jesus to the high priest. And all of the chief priests, elders, and scriptorians assembled.54 And Peter followed him from a distance, up to the high priestâs courtyard. And he was sitting with the guards and warming himself at the fire.55 But the chief priests and the whole Sanhedrin were seeking testimony against Jesus, to put him to death, but they were not finding any.56 For many were falsely testifying against him, but their testimonies were not consistent.57 And some, having risen up, falsely testified against him, saying,58 âWe heard him saying, âI will destroy this temple, the one made with hands, and in three days another oneânot made with handsâI will build.ââ59 And thus their testimony did not agree.60 And having stood up in the middle, the high priest questioned Jesus, saying, âDo you have no answer? What is it that these people testify against you?â61 But he was being silent and did not answer at all. Again the high priest was questioning him and says to him, âAre you the anointed one, the son of the Blessed One?â62 And Jesus said, âI am. And you will see the son of man âsitting at the right handâ of the Power and âcoming with the clouds of heaven.ââ63 But the high priest, having torn his clothing, says, âWhy do we still need witnesses?64 You heard the blasphemy. What do you decide?â And all judged him to be deserving of death.65 And some began to spit on him and to cover his face and hit him and to say to him, âProphesy!â And the guards slapped him.

Notes

14:53 And they led Jesus away to the high priest: and with him were assembled all the chief priests and the elders and the scribes: Although Mark doesnât identify him by name, the high priest would have been Caiaphas, who served from 18 to 36 CE (compare Matt. 26:3 and John 11:49).

The phrase âwith himâ is not in the best manuscripts.72 âAllâ is probably hyperbole.

14:54 And Peter followed him afar off, even into the palace of the high priest: and he sat with the servants, and warmed himself at the fire: Contra the KJV, Peter is with not servants but guards, probably including members of the arresting party. The image of Peter sharing a fire with them comes close to implying that he has joined with them; he is âafar offâ from Jesus but nestled close to the guards.

Here, the common word for âfireâ (Greek: pur) is not used but rather the word that is normally translated as âlightâ (Greek: phÅs). It thus likely has a symbolic connotation.

14:55 And the chief priests and all the council sought for witness against Jesus to put him to death; and found none: âCouncilâ refers to the Sanhedrin, or leading council.

Mark is clear that they are not seeking the truth about Jesus but rather are seeking evidence to justify his death.

14:56 For many bare false witness against him, but their witness agreed not together: The Mosaic law required two witnesses (Num. 35:30; Deut. 17:6).

It is perhaps surprising that Judas is not one of the witnesses; this supports the idea that Judasâs role was to reveal Jesusâ location, not his teachings.

This verse echoes the sentiment of Psalm 27:12, where false witnesses attempt to destroy the psalmist.

14:57 And there arose certain, and bare false witness against him, saying: Mark very clearly signals that the testimony is false; this framing is crucial to the interpretation of the next verse.

14:58 We heard him say, I will destroy this temple that is made with hands, and within three days I will build another made without hands: In this line, âwe heardâ is emphatic, so those who testify emphasize that they themselves have heard Jesus say this, which highlights the falseness of their testimony. Then, they emphasize the first occurrence of âI,â so that the stress is not on the destruction of the temple but rather on the fact that Jesus himself will destroy it.

Jesus spoke of the destruction of the temple in chapter 13, but he did not say that he himself would destroy it. Further, a different word for temple was used: chapter 13 referred to the hieron (a broader term for the entire temple complex, used in 11:11, 15, 27; 12:35; 13:1, 3; 14:49) while this verse uses naos (which narrowly refers to the sanctuary; this term is also found in 15:29 and 15:38). Jesus had referred to âthree daysâ in the context of his prophecies about his suffering and death (8:31; 9:31; 10:34), but he had not described the rebuilding of the temple this way or specifically referred to his body as a temple. Jesus had not used the âmade with(out) handsâ language either. Calling the current temple âmade with handsâ would have offended the council because it denies divine involvement in its creation; worse, the idea of something being made by (human) hands suggests idolatry (Lev. 26:1; Ps. 115:4).

In sum, this accusation reads like a confused scramble of Jesusâ teachings. One point of this scene is that peopleâs inability to understand Jesusâ teachings led, in a very literal sense, to his death. Because this statement is partially true, Mark has carefully framed the statement with three attestations of its falsity (see 14:56, 57, 59) so that the audience will not be confused.

Relationship to Jeremiah 26. The prophet Jeremiah was tried for claiming that the temple would be destroyedâand he did not even claim to be the one who would destroy it. The portion of Markâs audience who was familiar with this story would have recognized that this accusation aligns Jesus with this HB prophet.

14:59 But neither so did their witness agree together: It is difficult to determine precisely what did not agree, unless some of the witnesses said, âI will destroy this templeâ and others said, âI will build anotherâ and this was thought to contradict.73

Deuteronomy 19:15â19 relates penalties for those who give false testimony.

14:60 And the high priest stood up in the midst, and asked Jesus, saying, Answerest thou nothing? what is it which these witness against thee? 61 but he held his peace, and answered nothing: Jesusâ lack of response is described by an emphatic double negative.

Many HB texts emphasize the idea of the quiet suffering of a just person (Ps. 38:12â15; Isa. 53:7; Lam. 3:28â30).

Again the high priest asked him, and said unto him, Art thou the Christ, the Son of the Blessed: The word âthouâ is emphatic, scornfully emphasizing that Jesus is an unlikely Messiah.

âChristâ is the Greek word used to translate the Hebrew âMessiah,â with the underlying meaning of âthe anointed oneâ; the New Rendition uses âanointed oneâ to emphasize the link to the anointing story (14:3â9).

âBlessedâ is a common Jewish circumlocution to avoid saying the name of God, so the high priest is asking if Jesus is the Son of God. The use of âblessedâ here is ironic: the high priest is willing to blaspheme through his treatment of Jesus but wonât risk blaspheming by speaking the name of God.

14:62 And Jesus said, I am: Some ancient manuscripts read âyou say that I amâ instead of âI am.â The phrase âyou say thatâ is probably the result of scribes wanting to harmonize the text with Matthew 26:64 and Luke 22:70; it is unlikely to be original.74

âI amâ uses the same Greek words that the Lord used to self-identify to Moses (Ex. 3:14). As 14:63â64 will make clear, for Jesus to use this name was regarded as blasphemy. Jesusâ previous refusal to respond adds even more weight to his words here.

and ye shall see the Son of man sitting on the right hand of power: The idea of âseeingâ suggests that the high priest will know that Jesus has been vindicated. Jesusâ words prophesy that Godâs justice will override the decision of the Sanhedrin.

The reference to the Son of Man quotes Daniel 7:13 which, in the LXX, aligns the Son of Man with God (see the Notes on 2:10).

Jesusâ language about sitting on the right hand quotes Psalm 110:1. In this psalm, God invites the Messiah to sit on the right hand, which symbolizes being given a place of authority. Jesus adds âof powerâ to the quotation; this is a euphemism to avoid speaking the name of God. It is surprising that Jesus would use a euphemism for the name of God here when he has not done so elsewhere. Mark is strongly making the point that there are no grounds to accuse Jesus of blasphemy in this case.

and coming in the clouds of heaven: This concept also comes from Daniel 7:13. But in Daniel, the Son of Man is coming into heavenânot to earth. This is significant. While is it possible that Jesus is using the image in a context other than its original one, it is more likely that Jesusâ words are describing the Son of Manâs entrance into heaven. (While the high priest would not literally see this, the idea of seeing should probably not be pressed too literally; see also the Notes on 13:26.)

In the HB, only God travels on the clouds (Num. 10:34; Ps. 104:3; Isa. 19:1), so by claiming that the Son of Man will, Jesus opens himself again to blasphemy charges. Further, in Daniel 7:27, the Son of Man is worshiped, and so Jesus is suggesting that he will be an object of worship. Not only that, but the people on this very council will worship him.

There appears to be some tension between the last phrase, where Jesus was seated, and this phrase, where he is in motion. There are two possible solutions to this:

neither image should be read overly literally; both are metaphorical pictures of enthronement.

the image is of a chariot throne (Ezek. 1:10).

In either reading, the key issue is that Jesus is providing an image of ultimate vindication. This would have been important for Markâs audience to understand, at the moment when Jesus appearsâby earthly standardsâto be powerless before the Sanhedrin. But Jesus is not on trial in front of anyone who has the power to exercise control over his destiny.

Jesus has taught previously that when the righteous are brought before councils, the Spirit will tell them what to say (13:11). So Markâs audience would therefore assume that the Spirit is here speaking through Jesus as he addresses the council. This would add weight to his words.

14:63 Then the high priest rent his clothes, and saith, What need we any further witnesses?: Tearing clothing was a way of expressing horror and was probably a ritual response to blasphemy. (High priests are forbidden [Lev. 21:10] to rend their priestly robes, but he would not have been wearing those in his home in the middle of the night.)

The thrust of this statement is that Jesusâ words constituted blasphemy, so there is no reason to seek any more witnesses.

14:64 Ye have heard the blasphemy: what think ye?: Blasphemy is, most commonly, misuse of the divine name, but Mark is clear that Jesus has not used Godâs name here. The claim to be the Messiah was probably not considered blasphemy; others made this claim and were not deemed blasphemous. Jesusâ âI amâ saying could have easily been regarded as blasphemous, since this is the language the Lord uses to self-identify to Moses. Blasphemy might also have been found in the idea of sitting at the right hand of God and thus sharing power or in claiming to be the Son of Man.

The question here could have been presented by the storyteller to Markâs early audiences in such a way that would have encouraged them to contemplate for themselves what they thought of Jesus. If they were to do that, then the next line, condemning Jesus to death, would be all the more devastating.

And they all condemned him to be guilty of death: The âallâ may be hyperbole (contrast 15:43).

According to the law of Moses, death by stoning is the penalty for blasphemy (Lev. 24:13â14). Obviously, that is not what happens here. This suggests that the religious leaders lacked the authority to execute Jesus; instead, they hand him over to the Romans.

This verdict violates many trial rules under Jewish law, but it is not clear whether this is a formal trial or merely a hearing75 and whether those rules, which are only known from later documents, were operative in the first century.

14:65 And some began to spit on him, and to cover his face, and to buffet him, and to say unto him, Prophesy: and the servants did strike him with the palms of their hands: Some manuscripts omit the phrase âand to cover his faceâ;76 it seems to have been added to conform to Lukeâs account.

The âsomeâ who torment Jesus are members of the Sanhedrin. Mark later records that they turned Jesus over to the Romans because they âenviedâ him (15:10). Spitting conveyed contempt (Num. 12:14; Job 30:9â10). The demand to prophesy probably means that they mockingly want Jesus to identify who has hit him. Of course, Markâs audience is aware that many of Jesusâ prophecies have in fact come true in this scene, including his prediction that the religious leaders would mock him and spit on him (10:34). So the irony is overwhelming.

Analysis

The idea of a messianic secret in Mark is complicated, but to the extent that there was one, it ends with Jesusâ âI amâ statement to the high priest. In this context of persecution and suffering, Jesus can now reveal to all that he is the Anointed One (contrast 8:30). In these circumstancesâunlike after healing miraclesâwhen it requires faith to believe it, Jesus can proclaim that he is the Christ. The next line, with its focus on what they will see in the future in contrast to how things now appear, emphasizes this idea by showing Jesusâ future vindication.

With the condemnation to death, Jesusâ prophecy is fulfilled (10:33). The constant refrain of fulfilled prophecies in the Passion narrative make clear that, despite appearances, Jesus is in control.

Peter Denies Jesus (14:66â72)

66 And as Peter was beneath in the palace, there cometh one of the maids of the high priest:67 And when she saw Peter warming himself, she looked upon him, and said, And thou also wast with Jesus of Nazareth.68 But he denied, saying, I know not, neither understand I what thou sayest. And he went out into the porch; and the cock crew.69 And a maid saw him again, and began to say to them that stood by, This is one of them.70 And he denied it again. And a little after, they that stood by said again to Peter, Surely thou art one of them: for thou art a Galilaean, and thy speech agreeth thereto.71 But he began to curse and to swear, saying, I know not this man of whom ye speak.72 And the second time the cock crew. And Peter called to mind the word that Jesus said unto him, Before the cock crow twice, thou shalt deny me thrice. And when he thought thereon, he wept.

New Rendition

66 And while Peter was below, in the courtyard, one of the female servants of the high priest comes.67 And having seen Peter warming himself, having looked at him, she says, âAnd you were with the Nazarene, Jesus.â68 But he denied it, saying, âI donât know, or even understand, what you say.â And he went out onto the entryway.69 And the servant, having seen him, began again to say to those nearby, âThis is one of them!â70 But again he was denying it. And after a little while, again those nearby were saying to Peter, âSurely you are with them. For you are definitely a Galilean.â71 But he began to curse and to swear [an oath]: âI donât know this man you are talking about!â72 And immediately, a rooster crowed the second time. And Peter remembered what Jesus had said to him: âBefore the rooster crows twice, you will deny me three times.â And having broken down, he cried.

Notes

14:66 And as Peter was beneath in the palace, there cometh one of the maids of the high priest: Peter is not in a âpalaceâ but rather in a courtyard.

14:67 And when she saw Peter warming himself, she looked upon him, and said, And thou also wast with Jesus of Nazareth: âLooked uponâ implies that the woman did a double take. It seems that she has seen Peter with Jesus on some other occasion and thus recognizes him. It is possible that her statement is an observation and not an accusation, which makes Peterâs denial of Jesus even less understandable.

14:68 But he denied, saying, I know not, neither understand I what thou sayest. And he went out into the porch; and the cock crew: The evidence is mixed regarding whether the words âand the cock crewâ were in the early manuscripts of Mark.77 Without this line, it is as if neither Peter nor Markâs audience âhearsâ the first crowâthey only hear the second, when it is too late and Jesusâ prophecy (14:30) is fulfilled. Of course, the reference to the second crowing makes clear that there was a first, but Markâs story makes more sense if the first crowing is left unnarrated.

Peterâs words can be taken as a statement followed by a question (âI donât know or understand. What are you saying?â) or as one statement (âI donât know or understand what you are sayingâ). The former is less likely because Peter would not want to encourage further conversation. It is possible that Peterâs claim not to understand refers to her accent, but it is clear that he does indeed understand her. There is âexquisite ironyâ78 in that what Peter says is, in a sense, true: he actually does not know who Jesus is.

Peterâs move onto the porch is probably an effort to get away from the woman and to get out of the light by which she can see him.

14:69 And a maid saw him again, and began to say to them that stood by, This is one of them: This verse refers to the same maid mentioned previously (the word âagainâ makes that point).

This group probably included some of those who arrested Jesus; they would have been able to confirm the identification of Peter as âone of themâ and perhaps arrest him as well.

14:70 And he denied it again. And a little after, they that stood by said again to Peter, Surely thou art one of them: for thou art a Galilaean, and thy speech agreeth thereto: The phrase âand thy speech agreeth theretoâ is missing from many manuscripts. It appears to have been added to later copies of Mark either to clarify how Peter was recognized as a Galilean or to harmonize with Matthewâs account (Matt. 26:73).79

14:71 But he began to curse and to swear, saying, I know not this man of whom ye speak: To âcurseâ means to call down a curse upon someone if what is said is not true (compare 2 Sam. 3:9 and Acts 23:12). Normally, there would have been a direct object following it (that is, âto curse [name of person]â), but there is not in this case; it is possible that Peter cursed Jesus, but Mark chose to elide this fact. Because âcursing Jesus served as proof of innocence before a Roman tribunal when a person was accused of unlawfully being a Christianâ80 (not during Jesusâ time, but later and thus relevant for Markâs audience), it would have been reassuring for Markâs audience to know that even the lead disciple failed when faced with a similar test. Because they know of Peterâs later position, they would realize that even a setback as grave as this need not be permanent.

To âswearâ means to swear an oathâa promise that what is being said is true.

Notice that Peter expends a lot of effort to avoid saying Jesusâ name: he instead says, âthis man of whom ye speak.â

14:72 And the second time the cock crew. And Peter called to mind the word that Jesus said unto him, Before the cock crow twice, thou shalt deny me thrice: Although not included in the KJV, this verse begins with the Greek word for âimmediately,â which emphasizes that Jesusâ prophecy came true with precision.

Some ancient manuscripts omit the words âthe second time,â probably to harmonize with versions of Mark that omitted the crowing in 14:68.81 But the best reading probably includes âthe second time,â assuming that the first crowing happened but was left unnarrated.

In the ancient world, cocks âprimarily epitomized virile aggressionâ82 due to their association with cockfighting. Their crow was ânot a cry of submission or an empty vaunt of superiority, rather it is affirmation of the cockâs readiness to fight on to its death,â83 so when Jesusâ prophecy links Peterâs denial to the cock crow, Jesus has suggested that Peter will behave spinelessly at precisely the moment when courage to the death is expected. Because âvalue was also placed upon cocks which might lose but fight to the death anyway,â84 Peterâs unwillingness to stand up for Jesus is emphasized by way of contrast. It would have been considered honorable for Peter to die while defending Jesus, but this is not what happens. Jesusâ linking of Peterâs denial to the crowing of the cock is a profound example of Markan irony. Cocks that lost their fights were âthought to lose free status and are referred to as slaves,â85 so there is another layer of irony in the fact that it is a female slave who bests Peter here. It is also ironic that cocks were thought to be âvigilantly wakeful,â86 which the disciples were supposed to be but were not. It is as if with the crowing of the cock, Peter finally wakes up to what is happening, as the next line suggests.

And when he thought thereon, he wept: The precise meaning of the verb translated as âthought thereonâ is unknown. It may mean that Peter rushed outside. The New Rendition uses âbreaking down,â but this is uncertain.

Relationship to the Death of John the Baptist (6:14â29). Peterâs denial of Jesus and the story of the death of John the Baptist are the only stories in Mark that do not feature Jesus. However, they are very much about Jesus: both showcase the response of disciples under persecution. John, too, could have denied his testimony and been spared as Peter was. They further Markâs theme of discipleship by showing how disciples respond to threats to their lives.

Analysis

Peter denies Jesus three times; the denials escalate from a simple denial to one accompanied by a curse and an oath. This obviously reflects poorly on Peter, but it reflects even more on Jesusâ mercy: the end of the Gospel will make clear that Peter will be offered an opportunity to return to discipleship (16:7).

In chapter 13, Jesus taught the disciples that they would need to watch to be prepared to face the challenges of persecution. In the Gethsemane scene, Jesus repeated the command to watch. But the disciples did not; instead, they slept. Because they slept, they did not witness Jesusâ prayer. If they had, they would have known how to remain true in the face of suffering by placing Godâs will above their own. The triple repetition of watching in chapter 13 and in Gethsemane and the triple repetition of Peterâs denial suggest this link. In this scene, Peter fails as a disciple by denying Jesus because he did not place Godâs will first. In Markâs Gospel, the prayer in Gethsemane is the key to strong discipleship, serving as a model for the disciples.

At the same time that Peter is outside and denying Jesus to the high priestâs maid, Jesus is inside and identifying himself to the high priest; this juxtaposing encourages the hearer to compare Peterâs denial with Jesusâ confession. In the narrative, Peterâs denial happens at precisely the same moment that Jesus confesses himself, which shows in the starkest terms the difference between the two, especially since Jesus is being interrogated by the high priest while Peter is only being questioned by the high priestâs servant. (In fact, the irregularity of Jesusâ âtrialâ before the high priest may be shown by the fact that the high priest is doing no more than his serving woman, who also presents âevidenceâ and âwitnessesâ of Peterâs identity.) As some mock Jesus by ordering him to prophesy, his prophecy concerning Peter is simultaneously coming true in the courtyard. Indeed, both the act of testifying before rulers and his betrayal by his disciples have been prophesied by Jesus.

The crowing of the cock is clearly linked to Peterâs denials, but it is also, through the juxtaposing structure, linked to Jesusâ victory: the cock crow was associated with the proclamation of victory. Since Peterâs denial and Jesusâ confession happen at the same time, the cock crow also announces Jesusâ victory in submitting his will to the will of God. To an outside observer, it would appear that Jesus loses (since he is handed over to the Romans) and Peter wins (since he goes free), but the crowing of the cock shows that, ironically, it is Jesus who gains the victory by losing his life to save it. The audience is led to see victory in Jesusâ scene, despite all evidence to the contrary, through the crowing of the cock.

There are also several similarities between this juxtaposition and the previous one in chapter 14, where the anointing was sandwiched by references to the plot to kill Jesus. In both, someone who should be an insider (Judas, Peter) is outside betraying Jesus while Jesus is inside in a scene where his identity is confirmed. Perhaps most significantly, this reading draws a parallel between the woman and Jesus since both proclaim his identity. By repeating the sandwiching pattern, Mark emphasizes to the audience that even Jesusâ closest disciples failed him; further, the ironic link between the establishment of Jesusâ identity and the failure of the disciples is highlighted. Both sandwiched stories convey the idea that Jesusâ true identity as the Christ can only be understood in the context of his betrayal and subsequent suffering and death. He is clearly not the Messiah that they were expecting.

The term âPassion narrativeâ describes the accounts of Jesusâ suffering and death. (The word âPassionâ derives from the Latin for âsuffering,â passio.) It has become a clichÃ© to describe the Gospel of Mark as a Passion narrative with a lengthy introduction,1 but it is true that Mark grants disproportionate attention to the final days of Jesusâ life: about one-third of the text is devoted to Jesusâ final week and about one-sixth to his final day.

It is possible that the stories of the Passion narrative were told together before the Gospel of Mark existed, so the audience of Mark may have been familiar with this portion of the text (or, at least, a similar version of it).

Chapter 14, a lengthy chapter, contains some of the most significant events of Jesusâ story: his anointing, his observance of Passover, his prayer in Gethsemane, his abandonment and betrayal by his disciples, his arrest, his examination by the Jewish leaders, and Peterâs denial of him. While other options are possible, this is one proposal for the structure of this chapter:

1. Death Plot (14:1â2) 2.     Anointing of Jesus (14:3â9) 3. Death Plot (14:10â11)

4. Preparation for the Passover (14:12â16)

5. Prediction of Judasâ Betrayal (14:17â21) 6.     Last Supper (14:22â25) 7. Prediction of Peterâs Betrayal (14:26â31)

8. Preparation for the Passion (Gethsemane Prayer and Arrest) (14:32â52)

9. Peter Positioned to Deny Jesus (14:53â54)10.     Jesus Is Examined by the Sanhedrin (14:55â65)11. Peter Denies Jesus (14:66â72)

The chapter has three main scenes (14:3â9, 22â25, and 55â65); each one focuses on the topic of Jesusâ identity (see the Notes on those verses). Each is bracketed by reference to the betrayal of Jesus (14:1â2, 10â11, 17â21, 26â31, 53â54, 66â72). In between the three main scenes are scenes focused on the idea of preparation: first, the preparation for the Passover (14:12â16) and then preparation for the Passion (including Jesusâ arrest; 14:32â52). The framing of the Gethsemane scene in Markâs story of Jesus is noteworthy: it prepares Jesus to face his suffering and death, and it should have prepared the disciples as well.

In this structure, the main theme of chapter 14 concerns Jesusâ identity. Significantly, each foray into that identity (14:3â9, 22â25, 55â65) is literally surrounded by the idea of betraying Jesus. The fact that he is betrayed, abandoned, and denied by those closest to him is crucial to the way Mark presents Jesus and a significant component of Markâs message about discipleship.

The anointing of Jesus (14:3â9) is the centerpiece of Markâs Gospel, thus it is given extensive analysis in the commentary. For Mark, this is where Jesus literally becomes the Christ, since âChristâ means the Anointed One. It is not coincidental that Jesus says that wherever the gospel is preached, the story of his anointing must be told.

Jesus Is Anointed (14:1â11)
